Everyone else is nodding and moving on to the next agenda item, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what was just said for the last ten minutes. It’s just so exhausting because I care about my job, I've worked in healthcare management for years, I have my degree, I know how to do this... but I just sit there and completely lose the thread of what's right in front of me, and it's making me feel like I can't even trust myself to get through a basic afternoon anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, it's not like a dark cloud over my whole life or anything like that. Outside of work, I still enjoy things, mostly. Well, when I actually have the energy for them. My husband and I like to go to the farmers market on Saturday mornings, and I still look forward to that, even if sometimes I end up wandering away from the stall he's at because I get distracted by the artisan soaps or something. And I still love gardening—I have these hydrangeas that I'm incredibly proud of, they took three years to really establish because our soil is so sandy, but now they're just beautiful. So, no, the joy is still there. \n\nIt’s really just this... this intense frustration with my brain. It's not sadness, it's just feeling completely overwhelmed by my own inability to stay on track. Like, I’ll be trying to read a novel I was really excited about, and I'll find myself having to read the same paragraph four times because my mind started thinking about whether I remembered to submit that compliance form, or if I need to buy more dog food. So the interest in the book is there, I want to read it, I'm not feeling hopeless or down about life in general. It's just the mechanics of actually doing the things I want to do that feel broken, if that makes sense? I'm not crying myself to sleep, I'm just sitting at my desk wanting to pull my hair out because I can't force my brain to just do the thing it needs to do.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have trouble focusing in school or sitting still, or were you often described as restless, impulsive, or easily distracted?\n\nPatient: It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just talking to my sister about this not too long ago. We were going through some old boxes at my parents' house, and I found a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. And reading through them, it was like a broken record. Every single year, whether it was third grade or fifth grade, the teachers would write these notes in the margins saying that I was a very bright girl, that I had so much potential, but that I was just so careless. Careless! That was the exact word they always used. \n\nAnd I remember how much that used to sting, because I wasn't trying to be careless. I really wasn't. But my poor mother, oh my goodness, she basically had to make getting me through school her part-time job. Every single afternoon, she would have to sit right next to me at the kitchen table—we had this awful, scratchy oak table back then—just to make sure I actually finished my homework. If she got up to stir a pot on the stove or answer the phone, I would instantly be off in my own world, doodling on my folder or staring out the window. \n\nAnd that was assuming I even knew what I was supposed to be doing in the first place! Half the time, I would completely lose track of what the actual assignments were before the school bell even rang. Or I’d ride the bus all the way home, sit down with my mom at the table, open my backpack, and realize I had completely forgotten the math book or the spelling workbook I needed, and it was just sitting there in my desk at school. It was just a constant, exhausting cycle. My mom would get so frustrated, asking how I could possibly forget the one book I needed to bring home, and I never had a good answer for her. I just... forgot. So, yes, looking back, the trouble focusing was definitely there, even if we didn't really know what to call it back in the seventies and eighties. It was just who I was.\n\nDoctor: In your adult life, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or had trouble staying seated when you needed to? Or found yourself acting impulsively without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know some people with, well, you know, focus issues, they can't sit still for two seconds. My nephew is like that, actually. He's twelve, and if you try to make him sit through a family dinner, it's like he has springs in his chair. He's up, he's down, he's tapping his fork, he's asking to be excused before we've even passed the rolls. It's exhausting just watching him, honestly. But me? No, I don't have that problem. \n\nI can sit at my desk for hours. In fact, that's part of the problem, I just sit there staring at the screen, totally zoned out. I don't feel this urge to get up and pace around the office or anything. I might tap my pen every now and then, or shift in my chair, but who doesn't do that when they're stuck in a three-hour budget review meeting? And as for being impulsive... no, I'm actually a terrible over-thinker. I'll spend forty-five minutes agonizing over how to phrase an email to the nursing director because I don't want to sound too demanding, but I need the data by Friday. I don't just blurt things out or make snap decisions. If anything, I take too long to make decisions because I get so bogged down in all the details and what-ifs. So, no, the physical restlessness and the impulsivity, that doesn't really sound like me at all. It's really just the complete and utter inability to keep my brain on a leash.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you find yourself struggling to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like managing household tasks, following conversations, or keeping track of personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it definitely bleeds over into everything else. It’s not like I leave the office and suddenly my brain just snaps into sharp focus. I wish it did! But, you know, it’s funny you mention household tasks, because my husband and I actually had a pretty big argument about this just last week. I had promised—absolutely promised—that I would call the plumber about the guest bathroom sink, which has been draining slowly for, oh, gosh, probably two months now. And I kept meaning to do it. I would think about it while I was driving to work, or while I was brushing my teeth in our bathroom, but by the time I actually had a free minute to make the call, the thought was completely gone. Poof. Just vanished. And he was so frustrated, which I understand, I really do, because it feels like I’m just ignoring him or not pulling my weight, but it’s just that I literally cannot hold onto these tasks. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, either. It’s... well, it’s actually starting to have some real consequences, which is honestly why I’m here talking to you. At work, like I was saying before, the delays are getting worse. I manage a pretty busy outpatient clinic, and my inability to just sit down and finish the monthly reporting... it’s actually starting to affect our deadlines. Like, the regional director was asking for the quarterly patient volume metrics last Tuesday, and I had all the data, I just hadn't compiled it into the summary report because I kept getting sidetracked by emails and minor staff scheduling issues. So other teams are literally sitting around waiting on me, which is just mortifying. I’ve always prided myself on being professional, and now I’m the bottleneck. \n\nAnd then outside of work, I used to be really involved in these community health workshops we run on the weekends—mostly organizing the logistics, coordinating the speakers, that sort of thing. I loved doing it. But lately, I’ve actually had to step back from leading parts of it. I just... I couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. I would forget to confirm the venue booking, or I’d lose the email thread with the nutritionist who was supposed to speak, and then it would be three days before the event and I’d be scrambling, totally panicked, trying to patch it all together. It just got to be too much, the constant dropping of the ball. So I told them I needed to take a lesser role for a while. Which broke my heart a little bit, to be honest. It’s just so frustrating when you want to do these things, you have the desire and the capability, but your brain just won't cooperate with the actual execution of it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you also struggle with staying focused or organized in social situations or during leisure activities, like when you're out with friends, watching a movie, or trying to follow a conversation at a gathering?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Movies are nearly impossible for me unless it is something I have seen a hundred times. My husband will want to watch some complex thriller, and twenty minutes in, I am leaning over asking him who that character is, and he will look at me like I am crazy and tell me that guy was literally in the opening scene. So yes, sitting through a movie or a play, my mind just wanders off and I miss entire plot points. \n\nBut the conversation piece you mentioned... that is honestly the most embarrassing part of all of this. It happens socially, sure, if I am at a dinner party or a neighborhood get-together, I just completely lose the thread of what the person right in front of me is saying. But where it really, really gets me is in my professional life, which is just mortifying at my age and in my position. \n\nI will be sitting in my office, and when a medical director or one of my coordinators is talking directly to me—and I mean it is just the two of us, no external distractions, they are looking right at me—I’ll be looking at them and nodding. I do all the right things, I make perfect eye contact, I smile, I give every physical indication that I am deeply engaged in the discussion. But internally? My mind has just completely drifted away. I might be thinking about a budget variance report, or wondering if I left my travel mug in the staff lounge, or sometimes I am just completely blanking out, staring at the pattern on their collar. \n\nAnd then they finish speaking, and there is this expectant pause, and I realize I have no earthly idea what they just told me. I have to swallow my pride and ask them to repeat the action item because the words just didn’t stay with me. They just hit my ears and vanished into thin air. It is incredibly frustrating, because I respect these people, I genuinely want to hear what they are saying, but it is like my brain just refuses to hit the record button. I spend half my day apologizing and asking my colleagues to run through things one more time, and I know they must think I am either losing my hearing or just don't care about what they have to say, which couldn't be further from the truth! It is just so exhausting trying to force myself to stay present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting daily tasks or losing items necessary for activities, even when you’ve made an effort to remember them?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, yes. It’s... well, it's actually becoming a running joke with some of the clinic staff, which I pretend to laugh at, but inside I'm just absolutely cringing. I am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for things I need just to get through the day. \n\nTake my security badge, for instance. You need it to get into the administrative wing, the supply closets, the staff breakroom, everywhere. I will have it in my hand, I’ll set it down just for a second to pour a cup of coffee, and then ten seconds later I have no earthly idea where it went. And it's not just my badge, either. It's my handwritten notes from the morning huddles, or these thick, brightly colored clinic folders that you would think are completely impossible to lose because they are neon green, but somehow I manage it! \n\nJust the other day, I had this little silver flash drive. I literally just had it in my hand right before a big meeting with the department heads. I remember holding it, feeling the metal edge of it, thinking, okay, I have the presentation ready to go. I walked down the hall, walked into the conference room, sat down, reached to plug it into the laptop... and it was gone. Just vanished. I had to make everyone wait while I ran back to my office, checking my pockets, checking the floor, looking like a complete madwoman. I eventually found it sitting on top of the water cooler, of all places, but... \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. It really is. Because I didn't use to be like this. I used to be the organized one! When I first started out in healthcare administration, people came to me because I knew where everything was. My desk was immaculate, everything was color-coded, everything had a designated place. If you needed a file, I could hand it to you in three seconds flat. And now? Now I'm the one frantically patting down my pockets and tearing my desk apart looking for a folder I was holding literally two minutes ago. It just takes up so much of my day, the searching, the retracing my steps, trying to figure out where on earth my brain decided to set something down while I was distracted by something else... it's like a full-time job just keeping track of my own hands!\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying seated, even when you needed to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. Actually, it was quite the opposite when I was a little girl. I was never one of those kids who was bouncing off the walls or constantly fidgeting in my chair. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was this very strict, older woman who always wore these heavy, scratchy wool cardigans, even in the spring when it was getting warm—she actually used to praise me for being such a good, quiet girl. \n\nI could sit at my desk for hours. Physically, I mean. I wasn't running around the classroom or asking to go to the bathroom every five minutes just to walk the halls like some of the other kids did. There was this one boy in my class, Tommy, who literally could not keep all four legs of his chair on the floor to save his life, he was just constantly tipping back and forth, tapping his pencils, just in constant motion. But that just wasn't me at all. \n\nMy body was perfectly still. I would sit right there at my little wooden desk with my hands folded, looking straight ahead. Now, my mind... well, my mind was probably out the window, daydreaming about playing in the yard or wondering what my mom had packed me for lunch. But physically? No, I never had that urge to get up and move. I was perfectly content to just sit there. My mother actually used to tell her friends that I was her easiest child to take to church or to the doctor's waiting room, because you could just put me in a chair and I would stay exactly where you left me for the entire hour. \n\nSo, no, the physical restlessness, the feeling like you're driven by a motor or needing to get up and pace around... I really never experienced that at all as a child. It's funny, because when you hear about these kinds of focus issues, you always picture the little boy who can't stay in his seat, but I was just the quiet girl staring at the chalkboard not making a peep. So I definitely didn't have the physical restlessness part of it.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you found yourself feeling driven to move, unable to relax, or acting on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it’s exactly the same now as it was when I was a child. I really don't have that physical restlessness at all. I mean, if you look at my daily routine, I am probably too sedentary, if anything. If it weren't for my smartwatch buzzing on my wrist at ten minutes to the hour telling me it's time to stand up, I could easily sit at my desk for four or five hours straight without moving a muscle. \n\nJust last Thursday, for example, I was working on our quarterly compliance audits—which, let me tell you, the new state guidelines have made absolutely agonizing, it's just endless spreadsheets and cross-referencing—and I realized I had been sitting in the exact same position from one o'clock until well past five. I hadn't gotten up to use the restroom, I hadn't walked down the hall to refill my water bottle, nothing. I was just planted there. Now, I might have been staring blankly at the monitor for half that time, completely zoned out and thinking about my grocery list, but my body was perfectly still! So I definitely don't have that feeling of being driven by a motor or needing to burn off physical energy. \n\nAnd as for being impulsive... oh heavens, no. I am the exact opposite of impulsive. I am a chronic over-thinker. In healthcare administration, you really can't afford to make snap decisions anyway. Everything has a protocol, a committee, a risk assessment... you have to think five steps ahead before you do anything. Even in my personal life, if my husband suggests trying a new restaurant on a whim on a Friday night, I can't just say 'yes, let's go.' I have to look up the menu online, check the recent reviews, figure out what the parking situation is going to be like downtown because you know how awful the street parking gets near the arts district... it drives him a little crazy, to be honest, how much I have to plan every little detail. \n\nSo, no, acting without thinking just isn't in my nature at all. I don't blurt things out, I don't make rash decisions, and I definitely don't have trouble physically relaxing on the couch at the end of the day. My struggles are entirely just... the mental drift. The losing my train of thought, the misplacing my folders, the forgetting what my medical director just said to my face. The physical stillness and the overthinking? I have those down to an absolute science.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focus and organization in different settings, like at home, at work, and in social situations, or are they limited to just one area of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... you know, I really haven't thought about it in those terms. I'm honestly not sure. I mean, my brain is just so consumed with the clinic and the administrative side of things that work is really where I feel it the most, but... is it happening at home? Or socially? I really couldn't tell you for certain. \n\nBecause, well, at home, things are just so structured already. My husband and I have lived in the same house for fifteen years, and he is very much a creature of habit. He handles a lot of the day-to-day household routine now. Like, he always sorts the mail, he always puts the keys in that little ceramic bowl by the front door—the one my sister got us from her trip to Santa Fe, which I actually thought was a bit gaudy at first, with all these bright turquoise tiles, but it turns out it’s the absolute perfect size for keys and loose change. So, because he has those systems in place, I don't really have the opportunity to lose things the way I do at the office. If I set my sunglasses down somewhere weird, he usually just picks them up and puts them where they belong before I even realize they're missing. So I haven't really noticed it being an issue there, but maybe that's just because he's covering for me? I really don't know.\n\nAnd as for social situations... I'm really not sure what that would even look like, to be perfectly honest. We don't have a hugely active social life right now anyway, just because we're both so tired by the end of the week. If we do go out, it's usually just dinner with another couple, like our friends the Millers. And Sarah Miller is one of those people who plans everything down to the minute. She tells us what time to be there, what to bring... all I have to do is show up and drink a glass of wine. I suppose there was one time last month I completely lost the thread of the conversation while Sarah was talking about her daughter's college applications, but I just chalked that up to being exhausted from a long week of budget reviews. \n\nSo, I guess I really couldn't say for sure if it's bleeding into all those other areas of my life, or if it's just strictly a work problem. I'm just so hyper-focused on how much it's affecting my job right now, because that's where the stakes are, you know? That's where I have a whole team of people relying on me to know where the files are and what the medical director said. So outside of those four walls at the clinic, I honestly just haven't paid enough attention to give you a good answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your home life or social interactions where you’ve struggled with focus, forgetfulness, or losing things, even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the social side of things... I suppose, if you count the community volunteer work I do, that would definitely be an example. We have these volunteer health workshops that I've been involved with for years—it's a really wonderful program where we go out and do community education, basic health literacy, coordinating free screenings, that sort of thing. I used to be one of the main organizers. I loved doing it. But recently... well, I’ve actually had to step back from leading parts of those workshops. I just realized I can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving anymore. There's just so much to coordinate, you know, with the venues and the volunteers, and I kept losing track of the email threads or forgetting to follow up with the community centers until the absolute last minute. It was just getting too stressful, and I felt terrible because things were falling through the cracks, so I had to step down from the leadership role there. \n\nAnd honestly, that same exact feeling of holding people up is what's happening at the clinic, which is why it's weighing on me so heavily. I know you were asking about outside of work, but the work situation is just so pressing right now, it's hard to even separate the two in my mind. My delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines, which is a huge deal in healthcare administration. It’s not just me working late to catch up in my own little bubble anymore; I’m literally making other teams wait on me. The finance department, the clinical leads... they can't move forward with their monthly summaries or their budget forecasts until I submit my pieces. And I just sit there staring at the screen, knowing they need it, knowing the deadline is passing, but I just can't get my brain to organize the information and finish the task. So, yes, the delays are definitely causing real, tangible problems, both with my volunteer groups and with my colleagues... it's just becoming a very heavy burden to carry.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, like I said about the keys and that ceramic bowl, my husband does manage a lot of the household items, so I don't lose things like the TV remote as much. But... oh, I suppose the things I am personally responsible for carrying around, the things that are solely mine, that's where it really falls apart. It's just... it's mortifying. Because I know how it looks to the staff. They see me scrambling, looking for a folder or a flash drive, and I can see them thinking, 'what on earth is going on with her?' I used to have everything so perfectly managed, and now it feels like if an item isn't physically glued to my palm, it just vanishes into thin air the second I look away. So yes, misplacing things is a huge issue for me right now, even if it's mostly the things I need for my job.

It's funny, because when you hear about these kinds of focus issues, you always picture the little boy who can't stay in his seat, but I was just the quiet girl staring at the chalkboard not making a peep. So I definitely didn't have the physical restlessness part of it.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you found yourself feeling driven to move, unable to relax, or acting on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it’s exactly the same now as it was when I was a child. If it weren't for my smartwatch buzzing on my wrist at ten minutes to the hour telling me it's time to stand up, I could easily sit at my desk for four or five hours straight without moving a muscle. \n\nJust last Thursday, for example, I was working on our quarterly compliance audits—which, let me tell you, the new state guidelines have made absolutely agonizing, it's just endless spreadsheets and cross-referencing—and I realized I had been sitting in the exact same position from one o'clock until well past five. I suppose there was one time last month I completely lost the thread of the conversation while Sarah was talking about her daughter's college applications, but I just chalked that up to being exhausted from a long week of budget reviews. \n\nSo, I guess I really couldn't say for sure if it's bleeding into all those other areas of my life, or if it's just strictly a work problem. I'm just so hyper-focused on how much it's affecting my job right now, because that's where the stakes are, you know? That's where I have a whole team of people relying on me to know where the files are and what the medical director said. So outside of those four walls at the clinic, I honestly just haven't paid enough attention to give you a good answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your home life or social interactions where you’ve struggled with focus, forgetfulness, or losing things, even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the social side of things... I suppose, if you count the community volunteer work I do, that would definitely be an example. We have these volunteer health workshops that I've been involved with for years—it's a really wonderful program where we go out and do community education, basic health literacy, coordinating free screenings, that sort of thing. I used to be one of the main organizers. I loved doing it. But recently... well, I’ve actually had to step back from leading parts of those workshops. I just realized I can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving anymore. There's just so much to coordinate, you know, with the venues and the volunteers, and I kept losing track of the email threads or forgetting to follow up with the community centers until the absolute last minute. It was just getting too stressful, and I felt terrible because things were falling through the cracks, so I had to step down from the leadership role there. \n\nAnd honestly, that same exact feeling of holding people up is what's happening at the clinic, which is why it's weighing on me so heavily. I know you were asking about outside of work, but the work situation is just so pressing right now, it's hard to even separate the two in my mind. My delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines, which is a huge deal in healthcare administration. It’s not just me working late to catch up in my own little bubble anymore; I’m literally making other teams wait on me. The finance department, the clinical leads... they can't move forward with their monthly summaries or their budget forecasts until I submit my pieces. And I just sit there staring at the screen, knowing they need it, knowing the deadline is passing, but I just can't get my brain to organize the information and finish the task. So, yes, the delays are definitely causing real, tangible problems, both with my volunteer groups and with my colleagues... it's just becoming a very heavy burden to carry.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations at home or in your personal life where you've noticed the same kind of difficulty staying focused or keeping track of tasks, like misplacing everyday items or forgetting things you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said about the keys and that ceramic bowl, my husband does manage a lot of the household items, so I don't lose things like the TV remote as much. But... oh, I suppose the things I am personally responsible for carrying around, the things that are solely mine, that's where it really falls apart. And it usually happens right as I'm transitioning from home to the clinic, or moving around the office. \n\nIt is just so incredibly embarrassing, to be completely honest with you. I mean, I have been doing this for decades, and I used to be the organized one! People used to come to me when they couldn't find a policy manual from five years ago because they knew I'd have it tabbed and color-coded on my shelf. I was the person who kept everyone else on track. \n\nBut now? Now I am just constantly, and I mean *constantly*, hunting for things. My ID badge, for instance. I need it to swipe into the administrative suite, and I will have it in my hand in the car, and by the time I walk through the lobby and get to the elevator, it's just... gone. I'll be frantically digging through my purse, checking my coat pockets, sometimes even walking all the way back out to the parking garage just to find I dropped it on the floorboard. \n\nAnd it's not just the badge. I literally had it in my hand. I turned around to grab my coffee mug—the one with the little stethoscope on it that my niece bought me for my birthday—and when I turned back around, the flash drive was gone. I spent twenty minutes tearing my desk apart looking for a flash drive I *just* had in my hand before the meeting. \n\nIt's just... it's mortifying. Because I know how it looks to the staff. I used to have everything so perfectly managed, and now it feels like if an item isn't physically glued to my palm, it just vanishes into thin air the second I look away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, going all the way back to childhood... you know, it's funny you ask that, because it actually makes me think of this old cedar box my mother kept in the hall closet. She was the kind of person who kept absolutely everything—every macaroni necklace, every spelling test—but she especially held onto my old report cards. I remember looking through them years later, when we were helping her downsize, and there was this very distinct pattern, even way back in elementary school. 

I would read these little handwritten notes the teachers would leave in the margins, and it was almost always the exact same sentiment. They were constantly writing that I was a bright girl, very capable, but that I was just so careless with my work. And it wasn't for a lack of understanding the material, it was just the execution of it. 

And getting through homework at the house was... well, it was an absolute ordeal, to put it mildly. My mother literally had to pull up a chair and sit right there next to me at the kitchen table—we had this big, heavy round oak table—just to make sure I actually finished what I started. Because if she didn't sit there and actively monitor me, if she just told me to go do my homework in my room, I would completely lose track of what the assignments even were. I'd start on a math worksheet, get distracted by something out the window, and an hour later I'd be drawing horses on the back of my notebook and the math would be completely forgotten. 

That is, of course, assuming I even had the materials to do the work in the first place! Half the time, she'd sit down with me and ask to see my spelling list or my history book, and I would realize I had completely forgotten the books at school. I'd leave them in my desk, or I'd drop them in my locker and just walk home without them. She used to get so incredibly frustrated with me, bless her heart, because she knew I was smart enough to do the work, but the actual process of keeping track of the assignments and sitting down to finish them without her practically holding my hand was just nearly impossible. So, yes, looking back on it now, I suppose that exact same struggle to stay on track has been there since I was a little girl.

But lately, I can sit down at my desk to start an EPIC quality report, which is something I do all the time and is incredibly important for our clinic, or I'll sit down in one of our big clinic metrics meetings with the other department heads... and I go in with every intention of tracking it. I really do. I sit there, I have my notes ready, I tell myself, \"Okay, focus on this, this matters.\" \n\nBut then, within just a few minutes, my mind has drifted so far away. I don't even know where my brain goes half the time, but suddenly I’ll realize I’m just staring at my monitor, rereading the exact same row of numbers over and over again without actually processing what any of them mean. Or, if I'm in the metrics meeting, I'll kind of snap back to reality and realize I completely missed the whole point of the discussion. Everyone else is nodding and moving on to the next agenda item, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what was just said for the last ten minutes. It’s just so exhausting because I care about my job, I've worked in healthcare management for years, I have my degree, I know how to do this... but I just sit there and completely lose the thread of what's right in front of me, and it's making me feel like I can't even trust myself to get through a basic afternoon anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I feel depressed. I mean, it's not like a dark cloud over my whole life or anything like that. Outside of work, I still enjoy things, mostly. Well, when I actually have the energy for them. My husband and I like to go to the farmers market on Saturday mornings, and I still look forward to that, even if sometimes I end up wandering away from the stall he's at because I get distracted by the artisan soaps or something. And I still love gardening—I have these hydrangeas that I'm incredibly proud of, they took three years to really establish because our soil is so sandy, but now they're just beautiful. So, no, the joy is still there. \n\nIt’s really just this... this intense frustration with my brain. It's not sadness, it's just feeling completely overwhelmed by my own inability to stay on track. Like, I’ll be trying to read a novel I was really excited about, and I'll find myself having to read the same paragraph four times because my mind started thinking about whether I remembered to submit that compliance form, or if I need to buy more dog food. So the interest in the book is there, I want to read it, I'm not feeling hopeless or down about life in general. It's just the mechanics of actually doing the things I want to do that feel broken, if that makes sense? I'm not crying myself to sleep, I'm just sitting at my desk wanting to pull my hair out because I can't force my brain to just do the thing it needs to do.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have trouble focusing in school or sitting still, or were you often described as restless, impulsive, or easily distracted?\n\nPatient: It’s funny you ask that, because I was actually just talking to my sister about this not too long ago. We were going through some old boxes at my parents' house, and I found a stack of my old report cards from elementary school. And reading through them, it was like a broken record. Every single year, whether it was third grade or fifth grade, the teachers would write these notes in the margins saying that I was a very bright girl, that I had so much potential, but that I was just so careless. Careless! That was the exact word they always used. \n\nAnd I remember how much that used to sting, because I wasn't trying to be careless. I really wasn't. But my poor mother, oh my goodness, she basically had to make getting me through school her part-time job. Every single afternoon, she would have to sit right next to me at the kitchen table—we had this awful, scratchy oak table back then—just to make sure I actually finished my homework. If she got up to stir a pot on the stove or answer the phone, I would instantly be off in my own world, doodling on my folder or staring out the window. \n\nAnd that was assuming I even knew what I was supposed to be doing in the first place! Half the time, I would completely lose track of what the actual assignments were before the school bell even rang. Or I’d ride the bus all the way home, sit down with my mom at the table, open my backpack, and realize I had completely forgotten the math book or the spelling workbook I needed, and it was just sitting there in my desk at school. It was just a constant, exhausting cycle. My mom would get so frustrated, asking how I could possibly forget the one book I needed to bring home, and I never had a good answer for her. I just... forgot. So, yes, looking back, the trouble focusing was definitely there, even if we didn't really know what to call it back in the seventies and eighties. It was just who I was.\n\nDoctor: In your adult life, have you often felt restless, fidgety, or had trouble staying seated when you needed to? Or found yourself acting impulsively without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything like that. I know some people with, well, you know, focus issues, they can't sit still for two seconds. My nephew is like that, actually. He's twelve, and if you try to make him sit through a family dinner, it's like he has springs in his chair. He's up, he's down, he's tapping his fork, he's asking to be excused before we've even passed the rolls. It's exhausting just watching him, honestly. But me? No, I don't have that problem. \n\nI can sit at my desk for hours. In fact, that's part of the problem, I just sit there staring at the screen, totally zoned out. I don't feel this urge to get up and pace around the office or anything. I might tap my pen every now and then, or shift in my chair, but who doesn't do that when they're stuck in a three-hour budget review meeting? And as for being impulsive... no, I'm actually a terrible over-thinker. I'll spend forty-five minutes agonizing over how to phrase an email to the nursing director because I don't want to sound too demanding, but I need the data by Friday. I don't just blurt things out or make snap decisions. If anything, I take too long to make decisions because I get so bogged down in all the details and what-ifs. So, no, the physical restlessness and the impulsivity, that doesn't really sound like me at all. It's really just the complete and utter inability to keep my brain on a leash.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school, do you find yourself struggling to stay focused or organized in other parts of your life, like managing household tasks, following conversations, or keeping track of personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it definitely bleeds over into everything else. It’s not like I leave the office and suddenly my brain just snaps into sharp focus. I wish it did! But, you know, it’s funny you mention household tasks, because my husband and I actually had a pretty big argument about this just last week. I had promised—absolutely promised—that I would call the plumber about the guest bathroom sink, which has been draining slowly for, oh, gosh, probably two months now. And I kept meaning to do it. I would think about it while I was driving to work, or while I was brushing my teeth in our bathroom, but by the time I actually had a free minute to make the call, the thought was completely gone. Poof. Just vanished. And he was so frustrated, which I understand, I really do, because it feels like I’m just ignoring him or not pulling my weight, but it’s just that I literally cannot hold onto these tasks. \n\nAnd it’s not just at home, either. It’s... well, it’s actually starting to have some real consequences, which is honestly why I’m here talking to you. At work, like I was saying before, the delays are getting worse. I manage a pretty busy outpatient clinic, and my inability to just sit down and finish the monthly reporting... it’s actually starting to affect our deadlines. Like, the regional director was asking for the quarterly patient volume metrics last Tuesday, and I had all the data, I just hadn't compiled it into the summary report because I kept getting sidetracked by emails and minor staff scheduling issues. So other teams are literally sitting around waiting on me, which is just mortifying. I’ve always prided myself on being professional, and now I’m the bottleneck. \n\nAnd then outside of work, I used to be really involved in these community health workshops we run on the weekends—mostly organizing the logistics, coordinating the speakers, that sort of thing. I loved doing it. But lately, I’ve actually had to step back from leading parts of it. I just... I couldn’t reliably keep all the planning pieces moving. I would forget to confirm the venue booking, or I’d lose the email thread with the nutritionist who was supposed to speak, and then it would be three days before the event and I’d be scrambling, totally panicked, trying to patch it all together. It just got to be too much, the constant dropping of the ball. So I told them I needed to take a lesser role for a while. Which broke my heart a little bit, to be honest. It’s just so frustrating when you want to do these things, you have the desire and the capability, but your brain just won't cooperate with the actual execution of it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you also struggle with staying focused or organized in social situations or during leisure activities, like when you're out with friends, watching a movie, or trying to follow a conversation at a gathering?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Movies are nearly impossible for me unless it is something I have seen a hundred times. My husband will want to watch some complex thriller, and twenty minutes in, I am leaning over asking him who that character is, and he will look at me like I am crazy and tell me that guy was literally in the opening scene. So yes, sitting through a movie or a play, my mind just wanders off and I miss entire plot points. \n\nBut the conversation piece you mentioned... that is honestly the most embarrassing part of all of this. It happens socially, sure, if I am at a dinner party or a neighborhood get-together, I just completely lose the thread of what the person right in front of me is saying. But where it really, really gets me is in my professional life, which is just mortifying at my age and in my position. \n\nI will be sitting in my office, and when a medical director or one of my coordinators is talking directly to me—and I mean it is just the two of us, no external distractions, they are looking right at me—I’ll be looking at them and nodding. I do all the right things, I make perfect eye contact, I smile, I give every physical indication that I am deeply engaged in the discussion. But internally? My mind has just completely drifted away. I might be thinking about a budget variance report, or wondering if I left my travel mug in the staff lounge, or sometimes I am just completely blanking out, staring at the pattern on their collar. \n\nAnd then they finish speaking, and there is this expectant pause, and I realize I have no earthly idea what they just told me. I have to swallow my pride and ask them to repeat the action item because the words just didn’t stay with me. They just hit my ears and vanished into thin air. It is incredibly frustrating, because I respect these people, I genuinely want to hear what they are saying, but it is like my brain just refuses to hit the record button. I spend half my day apologizing and asking my colleagues to run through things one more time, and I know they must think I am either losing my hearing or just don't care about what they have to say, which couldn't be further from the truth! It is just so exhausting trying to force myself to stay present.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself forgetting daily tasks or losing items necessary for activities, even when you’ve made an effort to remember them?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, yes. It’s... well, it's actually becoming a running joke with some of the clinic staff, which I pretend to laugh at, but inside I'm just absolutely cringing. I am constantly—and I mean constantly—hunting for things I need just to get through the day. \n\nTake my security badge, for instance. You need it to get into the administrative wing, the supply closets, the staff breakroom, everywhere. I will have it in my hand, I’ll set it down just for a second to pour a cup of coffee, and then ten seconds later I have no earthly idea where it went. And it's not just my badge, either. It's my handwritten notes from the morning huddles, or these thick, brightly colored clinic folders that you would think are completely impossible to lose because they are neon green, but somehow I manage it! \n\nJust the other day, I had this little silver flash drive. I literally just had it in my hand right before a big meeting with the department heads. I remember holding it, feeling the metal edge of it, thinking, okay, I have the presentation ready to go. I walked down the hall, walked into the conference room, sat down, reached to plug it into the laptop... and it was gone. Just vanished. I had to make everyone wait while I ran back to my office, checking my pockets, checking the floor, looking like a complete madwoman. I eventually found it sitting on top of the water cooler, of all places, but... \n\nIt’s just so embarrassing. It really is. Because I didn't use to be like this. I used to be the organized one! When I first started out in healthcare administration, people came to me because I knew where everything was. My desk was immaculate, everything was color-coded, everything had a designated place. If you needed a file, I could hand it to you in three seconds flat. And now? Now I'm the one frantically patting down my pockets and tearing my desk apart looking for a folder I was holding literally two minutes ago. It just takes up so much of my day, the searching, the retracing my steps, trying to figure out where on earth my brain decided to set something down while I was distracted by something else... it's like a full-time job just keeping track of my own hands!\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often feel physically restless or have trouble staying seated, even when you needed to?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, not at all. Actually, it was quite the opposite when I was a little girl. I was never one of those kids who was bouncing off the walls or constantly fidgeting in my chair. I remember my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was this very strict, older woman who always wore these heavy, scratchy wool cardigans, even in the spring when it was getting warm—she actually used to praise me for being such a good, quiet girl. \n\nI could sit at my desk for hours. Physically, I mean. I wasn't running around the classroom or asking to go to the bathroom every five minutes just to walk the halls like some of the other kids did. There was this one boy in my class, Tommy, who literally could not keep all four legs of his chair on the floor to save his life, he was just constantly tipping back and forth, tapping his pencils, just in constant motion. But that just wasn't me at all. \n\nMy body was perfectly still. I would sit right there at my little wooden desk with my hands folded, looking straight ahead. Now, my mind... well, my mind was probably out the window, daydreaming about playing in the yard or wondering what my mom had packed me for lunch. But physically? No, I never had that urge to get up and move. I was perfectly content to just sit there. My mother actually used to tell her friends that I was her easiest child to take to church or to the doctor's waiting room, because you could just put me in a chair and I would stay exactly where you left me for the entire hour. \n\nSo, no, the physical restlessness, the feeling like you're driven by a motor or needing to get up and pace around... I really never experienced that at all as a child. It's funny, because when you hear about these kinds of focus issues, you always picture the little boy who can't stay in his seat, but I was just the quiet girl staring at the chalkboard not making a peep. So I definitely didn't have the physical restlessness part of it.\n\nDoctor: In the past six months, have you found yourself feeling driven to move, unable to relax, or acting on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, it’s exactly the same now as it was when I was a child. I really don't have that physical restlessness at all. I mean, if you look at my daily routine, I am probably too sedentary, if anything. If it weren't for my smartwatch buzzing on my wrist at ten minutes to the hour telling me it's time to stand up, I could easily sit at my desk for four or five hours straight without moving a muscle. \n\nJust last Thursday, for example, I was working on our quarterly compliance audits—which, let me tell you, the new state guidelines have made absolutely agonizing, it's just endless spreadsheets and cross-referencing—and I realized I had been sitting in the exact same position from one o'clock until well past five. I hadn't gotten up to use the restroom, I hadn't walked down the hall to refill my water bottle, nothing. I was just planted there. Now, I might have been staring blankly at the monitor for half that time, completely zoned out and thinking about my grocery list, but my body was perfectly still! So I definitely don't have that feeling of being driven by a motor or needing to burn off physical energy. \n\nAnd as for being impulsive... oh heavens, no. I am the exact opposite of impulsive. I am a chronic over-thinker. In healthcare administration, you really can't afford to make snap decisions anyway. Everything has a protocol, a committee, a risk assessment... you have to think five steps ahead before you do anything. Even in my personal life, if my husband suggests trying a new restaurant on a whim on a Friday night, I can't just say 'yes, let's go.' I have to look up the menu online, check the recent reviews, figure out what the parking situation is going to be like downtown because you know how awful the street parking gets near the arts district... it drives him a little crazy, to be honest, how much I have to plan every little detail. \n\nSo, no, acting without thinking just isn't in my nature at all. I don't blurt things out, I don't make rash decisions, and I definitely don't have trouble physically relaxing on the couch at the end of the day. My struggles are entirely just... the mental drift. The losing my train of thought, the misplacing my folders, the forgetting what my medical director just said to my face. The physical stillness and the overthinking? I have those down to an absolute science.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed these difficulties with focus and organization in different settings, like at home, at work, and in social situations, or are they limited to just one area of your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... you know, I really haven't thought about it in those terms. I'm honestly not sure. I mean, my brain is just so consumed with the clinic and the administrative side of things that work is really where I feel it the most, but... is it happening at home? Or socially? I really couldn't tell you for certain. \n\nBecause, well, at home, things are just so structured already. My husband and I have lived in the same house for fifteen years, and he is very much a creature of habit. He handles a lot of the day-to-day household routine now. Like, he always sorts the mail, he always puts the keys in that little ceramic bowl by the front door—the one my sister got us from her trip to Santa Fe, which I actually thought was a bit gaudy at first, with all these bright turquoise tiles, but it turns out it’s the absolute perfect size for keys and loose change. So, because he has those systems in place, I don't really have the opportunity to lose things the way I do at the office. If I set my sunglasses down somewhere weird, he usually just picks them up and puts them where they belong before I even realize they're missing. So I haven't really noticed it being an issue there, but maybe that's just because he's covering for me? I really don't know.\n\nAnd as for social situations... I'm really not sure what that would even look like, to be perfectly honest. We don't have a hugely active social life right now anyway, just because we're both so tired by the end of the week. If we do go out, it's usually just dinner with another couple, like our friends the Millers. And Sarah Miller is one of those people who plans everything down to the minute. She tells us what time to be there, what to bring... all I have to do is show up and drink a glass of wine. I suppose there was one time last month I completely lost the thread of the conversation while Sarah was talking about her daughter's college applications, but I just chalked that up to being exhausted from a long week of budget reviews. \n\nSo, I guess I really couldn't say for sure if it's bleeding into all those other areas of my life, or if it's just strictly a work problem. I'm just so hyper-focused on how much it's affecting my job right now, because that's where the stakes are, you know? That's where I have a whole team of people relying on me to know where the files are and what the medical director said. So outside of those four walls at the clinic, I honestly just haven't paid enough attention to give you a good answer.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of specific examples from your home life or social interactions where you’ve struggled with focus, forgetfulness, or losing things, even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about the social side of things... I suppose, if you count the community volunteer work I do, that would definitely be an example. We have these volunteer health workshops that I've been involved with for years—it's a really wonderful program where we go out and do community education, basic health literacy, coordinating free screenings, that sort of thing. I used to be one of the main organizers. I loved doing it. But recently... well, I’ve actually had to step back from leading parts of those workshops. I just realized I can’t reliably keep the planning pieces moving anymore. There's just so much to coordinate, you know, with the venues and the volunteers, and I kept losing track of the email threads or forgetting to follow up with the community centers until the absolute last minute. It was just getting too stressful, and I felt terrible because things were falling through the cracks, so I had to step down from the leadership role there. \n\nAnd honestly, that same exact feeling of holding people up is what's happening at the clinic, which is why it's weighing on me so heavily. I know you were asking about outside of work, but the work situation is just so pressing right now, it's hard to even separate the two in my mind. My delays have actually started affecting our clinic reporting deadlines, which is a huge deal in healthcare administration. It’s not just me working late to catch up in my own little bubble anymore; I’m literally making other teams wait on me. The finance department, the clinical leads... they can't move forward with their monthly summaries or their budget forecasts until I submit my pieces. And I just sit there staring at the screen, knowing they need it, knowing the deadline is passing, but I just can't get my brain to organize the information and finish the task. So, yes, the delays are definitely causing real, tangible problems, both with my volunteer groups and with my colleagues... it's just becoming a very heavy burden to carry.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situations at home or in your personal life where you've noticed the same kind of difficulty staying focused or keeping track of tasks, like misplacing everyday items or forgetting things you meant to do?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said about the keys and that ceramic bowl, my husband does manage a lot of the household items, so I don't lose things like the TV remote as much. But... oh, I suppose the things I am personally responsible for carrying around, the things that are solely mine, that's where it really falls apart. And it usually happens right as I'm transitioning from home to the clinic, or moving around the office. \n\nIt is just so incredibly embarrassing, to be completely honest with you. I mean, I have been doing this for decades, and I used to be the organized one! People used to come to me when they couldn't find a policy manual from five years ago because they knew I'd have it tabbed and color-coded on my shelf. I was the person who kept everyone else on track. \n\nBut now? Now I am just constantly, and I mean *constantly*, hunting for things. My ID badge, for instance. I need it to swipe into the administrative suite, and I will have it in my hand in the car, and by the time I walk through the lobby and get to the elevator, it's just... gone. I'll be frantically digging through my purse, checking my coat pockets, sometimes even walking all the way back out to the parking garage just to find I dropped it on the floorboard. \n\nAnd it's not just the badge. It's my handwritten notes from the morning huddle, or those thick clinic folders that I absolutely need to review. Just yesterday, I was preparing for a meeting with the IT department about our new patient portal, and I had this little silver flash drive with all the security protocols on it. I literally had it in my hand. I turned around to grab my coffee mug—the one with the little stethoscope on it that my niece bought me for my birthday—and when I turned back around, the flash drive was gone. I spent twenty minutes tearing my desk apart looking for a flash drive I *just* had in my hand before the meeting. \n\nIt's just... it's mortifying. Because I know how it looks to the staff. They see me scrambling, looking for a folder or a flash drive, and I can see them thinking, 'what on earth is going on with her?' I used to have everything so perfectly managed, and now it feels like if an item isn't physically glued to my palm, it just vanishes into thin air the second I look away. So yes, misplacing things is a huge issue for me right now, even if it's mostly the things I need for my job.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you often have trouble finishing tasks like schoolwork or chores unless someone was there to help you stay on track?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, going all the way back to childhood... you know, it's funny you ask that, because it actually makes me think of this old cedar box my mother kept in the hall closet. She was the kind of person who kept absolutely everything—every macaroni necklace, every spelling test—but she especially held onto my old report cards. I remember looking through them years later, when we were helping her downsize, and there was this very distinct pattern, even way back in elementary school. \n\nI would read these little handwritten notes the teachers would leave in the margins, and it was almost always the exact same sentiment. They were constantly writing that I was a bright girl, very capable, but that I was just so careless with my work. And it wasn't for a lack of understanding the material, it was just the execution of it. \n\nAnd getting through homework at the house was... well, it was an absolute ordeal, to put it mildly. My mother literally had to pull up a chair and sit right there next to me at the kitchen table—we had this big, heavy round oak table—just to make sure I actually finished what I started. Because if she didn't sit there and actively monitor me, if she just told me to go do my homework in my room, I would completely lose track of what the assignments even were. I'd start on a math worksheet, get distracted by something out the window, and an hour later I'd be drawing horses on the back of my notebook and the math would be completely forgotten. \n\nThat is, of course, assuming I even had the materials to do the work in the first place! Half the time, she'd sit down with me and ask to see my spelling list or my history book, and I would realize I had completely forgotten the books at school. I'd leave them in my desk, or I'd drop them in my locker and just walk home without them. She used to get so incredibly frustrated with me, bless her heart, because she knew I was smart enough to do the work, but the actual process of keeping track of the assignments and sitting down to finish them without her practically holding my hand was just nearly impossible. So, yes, looking back on it now, I suppose that exact same struggle to stay on track has been there since I was a little girl."
